Today, the streaming device and TV maker competes with often more advanced AI-powered connected TV products from rivals like Google and Amazon, powered by their assistants, Gemini and Alexa+. Alongside this update, Roku is making its newer, personalized Home Screen available in new markets, including Mexico and Brazil, where it will offer region-specific features like localized content and tailored suggestions. In addition, Roku’s Voice assistant will now be able to answer weather-related questions. On the streaming side, Roku will begin offering bundled subscriptions in the U.S. and Mexico that let you mix-and-match streaming plans from smaller services like AMC+, Fox ONE, Hallmark+, Howdy, and Starz. These bundles are usually more affordable than purchasing each plan à la carte. Roku also said it is open-sourcing the software that powers its Roku remote, Roku LT OS, which could invite outside developers and makers to build their own applications and devices. Inside the House That Suggests History Got Sad Spinster Lizzie Borden All Wrong
2026-09-17

Inside the House That Suggests History Got Sad Spinster Lizzie Borden All Wrong

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings After she was acquitted for the murders of both of her parents, Lizzie Borden did not adopt a pseudonym and disappear into obscurity. Instead, she spent her ample inheritance (equivalent to about $10 million dollars today) exactly as she’d always wanted to before her parents’ untimely demise: On an imposing 4,000-square-foot, seven-bedroom Queen Anne Victorian mansion in the swankiest neighborhood of her hometown of Fall River, Mass. She named her new abode “Maplecroft.” Borden—the subject of the latest installment of Ryan Murphy’s Netflix Monster anthology, which has previously dramatized the stories of Jeffrey Dahmer, the Menendez brothers, and Ed Gein—lived out the rest of her life at Maplecroft and died there almost exactly 100 years ago. The home’s a mere mile from the so-called murder house where Andrew and Abby Borden were famously axed to death in August 1892, when Lizzie was 32 years old. Some 50,000 true-crime fanatics flock annually to the macabre site (now a museum and bed and breakfast), but few know to trek up “The Hill,” as Fall River locals call it, for a glimpse of Borden’s other home. Now, as then, it’s a private residence whose owners would prefer looky-loos walk on. The exterior of the house, newly yellow after the Dohertys tested dozens of shades. Brooke Mullins Doherty Current owners Brooke and Michael Doherty bought the notorious property in 2021, reluctantly. “When Michael first showed me the house, I said I didn’t want to live in the house of a hatchet murderer,” admits Brooke, whose family previously lived 20 minutes away in a home they were quickly outgrowing. Maplecroft offered plenty of indoor and outdoor space, but with three young children in tow, they worried about unwanted publicity, strangers peeking through windows, and even ghosts haunting the halls. Brooke and Michael Doherty, current owners of Maplecroft, purchased the house in 2021, setting aside its eerie history in favor of its craftsmanship and ample space. Brooke Mullins Doherty But it was those hallways—and the grand and back staircases, six carved fireplaces, original crown molding, Tiffany sconces, and stained glass windows throughout—that kept the Dohertys returning to the French Street home. “Soon enough I was convinced I could absolutely live in this old Victorian house that was basically a museum,” says Michael. When the previous owner’s plans to turn Maplecroft into a bed and breakfast fell through during the pandemic, the asking price lowered to $800,000 and the Dohertys made a successful (undisclosed) offer. The front hallway of the home, which features its grand staircase. Brooke Mullins Doherty Borden moved in just three months after a jury of 12 men freed her from the 9-by-7-ft. cell at the county jail, having decided a mere woman couldn’t possibly have committed the murders. Her new digs were upgraded spectacularly from the much humbler home that was the scene of the infamous crime: Maplecroft was completely modern (in 1893, at least) and comparatively luxurious, with electricity, running hot and cold water, and flush toilets in all of its three-and-a-half bathrooms. Borden would later own one of the first automobiles in town, a black Ford Model T fittingly nicknamed the “Tin Lizzie,” which she stored in her carriage house—now Brooke’s art studio—with a private gas pump and customized turntable; America’s most notorious alleged murderer, it seems, couldn’t reverse park. The carriage house, where Borden parked her Model T, now serves as Brooke Mullins Doherty’s art studio. Brooke Mullins Doherty Fabulous real estate aside, popular culture paints post-trial Borden as a lonely outcast who was shunned by her neighbors and taunted by local kids who threw rotten eggs. But that’s not quite true: Borden lived at Maplecroft, for a time, with her sister Emma, nine years her elder, and their many pets—three Boston Bull Terriers and a black cat named Daisy. She had few if any friends among the townspeople, who had come to a slow acceptance, despite the jury’s verdict, that their Sunday School teacher had almost certainly committed the gruesome crime. Borden rarely ventured out except to go to the theater, where she could sit unnoticed in the darkness, and in 1904 the spinster fell into and found camaraderie with an inarguably more interesting bunch: the roving and booming Massachusetts theater community, including famous starlet Nance O’Neil, with whom Borden developed a close friendship. Since she was shunned anyhow, Borden had slowly stopped complying with rigid social rules and norms. She didn’t attend church and quit her previously prominent role in the Woman’s Christian Temperance Union. Both disapproved whole-heartedly of Borden’s rowdy theater friends, whom polite society considered similarly disreputable and immoral. Behind her tall fence, Borden hosted loud, lavish parties in Maplecroft’s basement, decked out as what the Dohertys call a speakeasy with, in Brooke’s words, “a nice wood floor big enough for dancing and floor-to-ceiling wainscoting wood panelling.” One latched panel, meanwhile, conceals a shallow hidden cupboard ideal for a Prohibition-era party. “Just enough space to hide a few whiskey bottles,” adds Michael. The basement where Borden once hosted parties and stored liquor in the walls, now serves, in part, as a space for laundry. Joshua Chopy Whatever went down in the basement, prudent Emma upstairs didn’t approve of the shenanigans. With the Fall River Herald reporting on the sisters’ every sighting (even their clothing—Emma wore all black in eternal mourning while Lizzie defiantly refused to wear black as decorum dictated), Emma thought they should live discreetly. Lizzie disagreed and, following a particularly offensive week-long bash in 1905, the eldest Borden sister packed her bags and left forever. “The happenings at the French Street house that caused me to leave, I must refuse to talk about,” Emma later told the Boston Post before speaking rather freely: “I didn’t go until conditions became absolutely unbearable.” On the advice of their reverend, Emma cut ties with her sister and moved away. They never met or spoke again, but died days apart in 1927 and were buried next to one another in the family plot. Upon her death, the childless Lizbeth Borden (as she called herself in later years) gifted property, jewelry, books and furniture to some distant friends and family members but left most of her money to Fall River’s Animal Rescue League. To the Dohertys, it’s all more evidence that their home’s previous owner was kind and generous, a woman with good taste and an appreciation for fine workmanship, but most importantly—despite what most Borden historians and public opinion and Ryan Murphy says—an innocent one. “The longer we live here,” says Brooke, “the more we’re convinced she didn’t do it.” Borden named her home and had it engraved in these stone steps, which remain to this day. Rebecca F. Pittman Maplecroft features a wraparound porch, added by Borden, where she spent time hanging out with her animals despite the taunts of neighborhood children. Joshua Chopy Maplecroft’s dining room, where Lizzie might have entertained had she had more fancy friends, with the original blue floral wallpaper she selected. Brooke Mullins Doherty An engraving carved above the fireplace reads: “And old-time friends, and twilight plays, and starry nights, and sunny days come trooping up the misty ways when my fire burns low.” Brooke Mullins Doherty The library, which today houses not only books but toys, instruments, and worldly decor. Brooke Mullins Doherty One of two bedrooms Borden used in the home; one was her winter bedroom, the other, her summer bedroom. Joshua Chopy A vintage flush toilet in the bathroom. Maplecroft’s modern amenities, including indoor plumbing, were a major upgrade from her previous residence, where the family used chamber pots. Joshua Chopy Lizzie Borden’s Maplecroft, as seen from above, where she lived until her death in 1927. Joshua Chopy

Carney Says Canada and E.U. 'Are Not Fair Weather Allies' as Trump Threatens Europe Over 'Hostile Act'

Carney Says Canada and E.U. 'Are Not Fair Weather Allies' as Trump Threatens Europe Over 'Hostile Act'

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump and Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney speak at the G7 summit in France on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney on Thursday welcomed the prospect of Canada becoming an “associate member” of the E.U., just hours after U.S. President Donald Trump dismissed the proposal as “laughable.” Addressing the European Parliament as a guest of honor in Strasbourg, France, Carney insisted “Europe and Canada are stronger together” and said “Canada and the European Union can anchor our ground to hold our values while forming a protective canopy under which our citizens can thrive.” Carney’s pivot toward Europe comes as he seeks to establish stronger international ties and curtail Canada’s reliance on the American market amid an all-out trade war. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last week, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Trump’s repeated threats against Canadian sovereignty and calls to make it the “51st state” have added to the months-long tension. While Carney did not directly mention Trump or his Administration while addressing European lawmakers, he appeared to make nods to the current volatility of Canada-U.S. relations and condemned economic coercions. Speaking about the "current geopolitical rupture," he said a "new dimension was needed.” “Economic integration is now being weaponized, tariffs being used to exert pressure,” he said. “Financial mechanisms have been used for coercion. Supply chains constitute weak points to be exploited.” Championing the burgeoning E.U. alliance, Carney focused on what he said were shared values between Canada and the bloc, insisting: "We are not fair-weather allies. We believe that our ​prosperity grows when ⁠it is shared." “I'm not proposing a third bloc in order to become a 'great power' rival, only with better manners,” Carney continued. “We do not seek power to dominate others. On the contrary, we are pursuing resilience so no one can control our open markets, impair our sovereignty, threaten our territorial integrity, or undermine our freedoms, our democracies, our rule of law.” Carney, who began his speech in French—one of Canada’s official languages—before continuing in English, with splatterings of German, welcomed how European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen had on Wednesday opened the door for Canada becoming an “associate member” of the 27-member bloc. When speaking about forging closer ties with the E.U., Carney referenced seeking strategic autonomy through “deep co-operation in the full range of strategic capabilities, including critical minerals, defense industrial capacity, AI and compute, energy security, space and payments.” He insisted Canada and Europe must not be dependent on “individual nations or individual companies” as this “creates vulnerabilities, particularly when supposedly global companies prove national in the breach.” He also hinted at a potential freedom of movement between the E.U. and Canada that would allow “youth to work, study, and live where they want, on either side of the Atlantic.” Carney’s passionate advocacy for the deepening of ties with the E.U.—a move he says Canadians have shown they’re in support of—came after Trump lambasted the idea of a new European dawn for Canada. Speaking to reporters Wednesday, Trump called the proposal “laughable” and threatened economic punishments if the E.U. moves forward with von der Leyen’s plan. "If they do that, if I think it's at all a hostile act, I will put very serious ​tariffs or stop trading with Europe on many things," Trump said en route to an event in North Carolina. "If ⁠it's a good intention, that's fine. If it's a bad intention, we'll put very heavy tariffs on Europe.” In response to Trump’s remarks, a European Commission spokesperson told TIME: “As president von der Leyen made clear [on Wednesday], the proposed strengthening of our partnership with Canada is not against anyone else, but for our common strength.” Carney's speech to the European Parliament came on the final day of his stay in Europe, where he sought to cement and deepen Canada’s international relationships. The E.U. is Canada’s second-largest global trading partner for goods and services, with bilateral trade worth approximately C$178.6 billion in 2025. The Canadian leader also traveled to Liverpool, England, to meet British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. The pair attended a football game and watched Everton—the team they both support—secure a victory against the Wolverhampton Wanderers. "The leaders began by underscoring the close co-operation between the U.K. and Canada and emphasized the importance of building similar close ties with European partners, particularly on trade and defence,” a Downing Street spokeswoman told TIME. Ahead of the meeting, Burnham called Carney a “great progressive leader” with whom the U.K. shares “so many values, but also with whom we have a shared opportunity on defense co-operation, on trade” and AI. Following Burnham and Carney's meeting, it was announced that Canada has applied to join the British-led Joint Expeditionary Force military coalition. The force, formally launched in 2014, consists of 10 North Atlantic NATO member states that deliver rapid response and security operations. “Prime Minister Carney announced that Canada has formally applied to join the Joint Expeditionary Force and welcomed the U.K.’s support for Canada’s participation in this important initiative,” Carney’s office said in a statement. Canada, already a NATO member, is seeking to diversify its defense dependency from the U.S. with new partnerships across the Atlantic. In February, Canada became the first non-European country to participate in the "Security action for Europe” (SAFE) instrument, which provides up to €150 billion in low-interest loans to help member states purchase military and defense equipment. Following von der Leyen’s proposal, a European Commission spokesperson told TIME that the executive cabinet will work “in close coordination with [its] member states and the European Parliament” to “create a new form of partnership that has not existed until now–building the closest imaginable form of association with a partner outside Europe.” The proposed alliance is set to include deeper economic ties, a technology alliance, greater integration of defense industrial bases, and efforts to make the Arctic a flagship joint project. It could also expand cooperation on energy, critical minerals and batteries, as well as AI, quantum technology, cybersecurity, and economic security. The E.U.-Canada summit set to take place in Montreal in late October will “provide the first meaningful opportunity to discuss these issues further,” the spokesperson told TIME. The proposal has been welcomed by many European lawmakers across the bloc. Member of the European Parliament (MEP) for Spain Javier Moreno Sánchez, who also serves as chair of the parliament’s delegation for relations with Canada, told TIME the decision to bring Canada in as an “associate member” was “politically and symbolically significant,” but warned it “must now be followed by concrete actions.” Irish MEP Barry Andrews told TIME that the announcement is a clear “win-win for both sides,” adding that “in an ever more insecure world, truly democratic countries must come together and stand up to Trump, [Chinese President] Xi Jinping, and [Russian President Vladimir] Putin.” Germany, the E.U.’s largest economy, also embraced the move, with government spokesperson Stefan Kornelius saying Berlin “welcomes the initiative for a particularly close partnership with Canada.” The E.U., much like Canada, is seeking new partnerships amid a weakening alliance with the U.S. under Trump, following disputes over tariff threats, his lashing out at the NATO alliance,  and his push to annex Greenland. Espen Barth Eide, Norway’s Foreign Affairs Minister, told TIME the world is "living through the most serious security situation since the Second World War.” “At a time like this, it is welcome that the E.U. is strengthening cooperation on European security. We also welcome the E.U.’s efforts to involve Norway, the United Kingdom, Canada and Iceland. Such an approach strengthens European security,” he said. Norway and Iceland are not members of the E.U., but both belong to the European Economic Area (EEA), giving them close economic ties to the bloc through trade and free movement—a model from which Canada could draw inspiration. Elsewhere, the “associate” membership proposal faced backlash. French MEP Manon Aubry, co-chair of The Left group, told TIME there was “a serious question of method,” arguing that “a decision of such magnitude cannot be made by the president of the European Commission alone.”

Dana Awartani
2026-09-17

Dana Awartani

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Can art offer a blueprint for healing? That’s the argument Dana Awartani, 39, makes in her work. The Saudi Arabian artist creates immersive installations of silk panels by ripping and “darning” (or repairing) the fabric. She also works with experts in ancient craft traditions to build installations that double as memorials to places destroyed by conflict. At this year’s Venice Biennale, she covered the floor of Saudi Arabia’s pavilion with 29,000 clay bricks, handmade by 32 master artisans, which referenced 23 damaged sites across the Levant. Entering the installation felt like being transported into an archaeological dig. Laid without binding agents, the floor was designed to crack over the course of the show’s run—an acknowledgement of the fragility of cultural heritage and the violence of erasure. Awartani, who trained in Islamic art traditions, will open solo shows at Lisson Gallery in London in November and Norway’s Stavanger Art Museum next year.

Ken Griffin
2026-09-17

Ken Griffin

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
“My first love in art was the proverbial one that got away,” says Ken Griffin, the CEO of the hedge fund Citadel. In 1999, he lost out on a sculpture of a dancer by the French Impressionist Edgar Degas. He has rarely let it happen again. Over the past two decades, Griffin has emerged as one of the world’s most dogged, deep-pocketed collectors. His treasures include Abstract Expressionist paintings worth nine figures, rare historical documents, and a Madonna and Child attributed to Leonardo da Vinci. While Griffin previously lent many of his most valuable works to museums anonymously, he has expanded and become more public about his activities over the past year, a shift he says is designed to facilitate scholarship and education as well as satisfy public curiosity. He loaned the da Vinci to the Met this spring, doubling the number of paintings by the Renaissance master on view in the U.S. The billionaire has also expanded his support of Florida museums since his much-publicized relocation to Miami from Chicago in 2022. Over the summer, an exhibition of 10 works by Jean-Michel Basquiat from his collection went on view at the Pérez Art Museum in Griffin’s new hometown. In 2027, he will place a Richard Serra sculpture he bought earlier this year on public display in a park next to the museum. “It’s monumental, it's thought-provoking, and, bluntly, it's just fun,” Griffin says of the work. “An institution has a different criteria to collect than I do as a private individual. I place a huge importance on the aesthetic appeal of a work. Does this work visually make me happy?”

Luke Nikas

As the amount of money being spent by the art world has risen over the past 20 years, the number of lawsuits—over botched business deals, alleged fakes, and Nazi loot—has grown in tandem. Luke Nikas has emerged as the art world’s go-to lawyer for some of its most bitter disputes. In the past decade, the partner at the firm Quinn Emanuel Urquhart & Sullivan has played key roles in a scandal over approximately $70 million worth of fake Modernist paintings sold by Knoedler Gallery; a messy breakup between art advisers to the stars; a wrongful-termination suit brought by the former director of the Philadelphia Museum of Art; and a fight over the legacy of Robert Indiana, the late artist who created the famous “LOVE” sculpture. In April, Nikas secured a $102.2 million jury verdict for his client, Indiana’s former business partner, after an eight-year-long dispute with an art publisher over who controls the late artist’s legacy and can profit off his work. The more litigious the art world becomes, the busier Nikas gets.

Noah Horowitz

Noah Horowitz

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Without art fairs, today’s highly globalized and commercialized art world would not exist. While many galleries complain about the high cost and grueling travel required to participate in international trade shows, they also rely on them to expand their clientele and turn buying art into an event. As chief executive of Art Basel, the most famous brand in art fairs, Noah Horowitz understands this paradox well. Since he took on the role in 2022, he has doubled down on Art Basel’s core business, most visibly by allying with the Qatari royal family to launch the firm’s fifth annual fair in Doha this past February and shepherding its Paris show to the historic Grand Palais in 2024. But he has also expanded the brand’s reach in forward-looking ways, including by augmenting its fairs with the dedicated digital art section Zero 10 and a retail shop for branded and artist-created merchandise, as well as diversifying Art Basel’s partnerships lineup with big names like Miu Miu, Samsung, and Qatar Airways. “I felt there was more Art Basel could do proactively and creatively… to meet an evolving market where it was,” Horowitz says. Now, he and his team are “working double time” to get there.

Kash Patel Refuses to Take a Republican’s Lifeline
2026-09-16

Kash Patel Refuses to Take a Republican’s Lifeline

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. President Trump appointed Kash Patel to lead the FBI not because of his brief experience in law enforcement but because he is a political brawler. The problem, as Patel demonstrated yesterday, is that he is not a very good one: No skilled political messenger would have allowed nearly every headline about a Senate hearing to connect their name and agency with bestiality. Yesterday’s Senate Judiciary Committee hearing was Patel’s first appearance on Capitol Hill since a raucous visit in May. (At that hearing, Patel and Senator Chris Van Hollen verbally agreed to each take a self-screening test for alcohol abuse. Van Hollen posted his results the next day, but his office said it never heard back from Patel.) It was also Patel’s first hearing since CBS News revealed changes to FBI hiring standards, which reportedly now allow the bureau to consider applicants who have hired prostitutes fewer than three times, stole from an employer more than three years ago, or engaged in sex with animals before the age of 18. Democrats were eager to press Patel on the changes, but the hearing’s buzziest exchange turned out to be between Patel and Senator John Kennedy of Louisiana, a Republican. During his questioning, Kennedy seemed to be doing everything he could to offer Patel a chance to defuse the story—but Patel, unable or unwilling to recognize the openings, didn’t take them. The ranking Democrat, Senator Dick Durbin of Illinois, asked about the new requirements first. Patel said the bestiality change was designed to ensure that people who had been forced to engage in sex with animals were not shut out of an FBI job. “We did not want to punish victims of bestiality,” Patel said. “We are not going to target victims.” Whether this is accurate is unclear. The memo laying out the new standards has not been made public, but sources told CBS News that the rules “do not distinguish between candidates who were coerced into sexual activity with animals, as opposed to those who engaged in such acts willingly.” Kennedy was the next senator to quiz Patel, and he tried to do some cleanup. “I’m really trying to understand,” he said, rubbing his face. “’Cause I listened to the exchange between you and Senator Durbin, and I’ve read a few news accounts, and I’m confused.” He asked Patel, almost rhetorically, to affirm that the FBI still had hiring standards, and Patel argued that changes had been necessary to ensure the quick hiring of good agents. As for bestiality, the old rule had “automatically disqualified any individual who participated on either side of it.” Kennedy, a bit of a ham, couldn’t resist a jibe: “So you disqualified the animal?” But he was also losing patience. “I’m trying to understand why you did this,” he said, and then offered an unusually frank description of how many Senate hearings work today: “I understand the game. People try to make you look bad. We do the same thing when the other party’s in office.” He gave Patel another shot. The director replied that sometimes human traffickers force victims to engage in bestiality, and that the FBI did not want to disqualify victims—even though, again, the guidelines reportedly don’t make such a distinction. An exasperated Kennedy wasn’t buying it. “Someone came to you and recommended” this? he asked. “And when you saw bestiality—I say this with respect—the person who recommended it, why didn’t you just say, ‘What planet did you parachute in from? Why do I wanna lead with my chin and get into bestiality?’” One answer is that Patel just isn’t very politically astute. It’s hard to claim you’re cleaning up and reforming the FBI, as Patel has, when you’re allowing johns and people who stole from their employers to apply. As for bestiality, more careful wording of the FBI memo that first laid out the new standards would have avoided the controversy. Better preparation for the hearing might have given him a clearer explanation than what he offered. And sharper instincts would have told Patel that Kennedy was trying to help him out. Any high-level appointee should be able to get through a congressional hearing without bestiality becoming the main topic. Instead, Patel takes his cues from Trump, whose instinct is always to push back. But what works for Trump doesn’t work for others. It’s not even clear whether it works for Trump anymore. Patel may have felt a need to loosen qualifications for agents because the FBI has experienced an exodus of staffers since he took over. More than 1,100 special agents, who had an average tenure of almost 22 years, left in 2025 alone, according to data from the Office of Personnel Management. Durbin has alleged that more than 5,000 nonpartisan civilian staffers departed last year. Some left of their own accord, and others were fired who were involved in investigations that Trump and Patel don’t like, such as those looking into the January 6 riot. This spring, my colleague Sarah Fitzpatrick reported that Patel’s colleagues had been concerned about what they described as Patel’s excessive drinking, repeated absences, emotional outbursts, and impulsive behavior. (Patel denied the allegations and sued Fitzpatrick and The Atlantic for defamation, demanding $250 million.) If Patel doesn’t have the skills and temperament to run the FBI well, isn’t really cleaning up the bureau’s ranks, and can’t even get through a standard Senate hearing without things going off the rails, one might wonder why he’s in the job at all. Lower hiring standards apparently start at the top. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: Days after two deadly earthquakes struck northern Venezuela on June 24, killing at least 6,000 people and injuring tens of thousands more, the Venezuelan Seismological Research Foundation, known as Funvisis, made an unusual public plea: Could whoever had taken one of its GPS sensors please give it back? For 13 years, the high-precision instrument had been bolted to a rocky outcrop in the hills of western Venezuela, quietly tracking the millimeter-scale movements of the Boconó fault, which was the likely source of the first of the two quakes. When Funvisis technicians went to recover the readings the sensor had stored, they found that the machine had gone missing. More than two months after the earthquakes, the organization still does not know who carried it off. Reminisce. Annie Dillard, who died yesterday at 81, believed that hearing the natural world is its own form of prayer, Isabel Fattal writes. Explore. Lily Meyer writes about an ingredient that’s all too rare in adult life: Whatever happened to being silly? Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. What Nature’s Silence Said to Annie Dillard
2026-09-16

What Nature’s Silence Said to Annie Dillard

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
“Nature’s silence is its one remark,” Annie Dillard wrote in a 1981 Atlantic essay. Dillard, who died yesterday at age 81, spent her life listening. She opens that essay, “Teaching a Stone to Talk”—which was later adapted into a collection by the same title—with the story of a man named Larry who is trying to get a rock to speak to him. “He keeps it on a shelf,” she writes, protected “by a square of untanned leather, like a canary asleep under its cloth. Larry removes the cover for the stone’s lessons.” Dillard is not sure what exactly happens at these lessons, but she respects the process: “It is a noble work, and beats, from any angle, selling shoes.” To Dillard, striving to hear the natural world was holy work too. “What have we been doing all these centuries but trying to call God back to the mountain, or, failing that, raise a peep out of anything that isn’t us?” she asks. Born in 1945 in Pittsburgh, she found inspiration in some of the places she lived. At age 28, she published Pilgrim at Tinker Creek, a Pulitzer Prize–winning nonfiction book that draws from her walks in the Blue Ridge Mountains in Virginia in the ’70s. Her musings on these solitary experiences in nature drew comparisons to Henry David Thoreau, who strongly influenced her work. She was also one of the first women to find a place in the male-dominated genre of wilderness writing. The writer Diana Saverin, who spent time going through an archive of Dillard’s personal writings and correspondence at Yale, once described in The Atlantic Dillard’s trepidation about how she would be received: “Before she published her book, she scribbled in her journal, wondering who would take her book seriously if its author was a ‘Virginia housewife named Annie.’” She dealt with this problem by leaving her day-to-day suburban life out of her work, so much so that some critics felt that she was exaggerating just how often she was in the wildness of nature. As Saverin noted, Dillard acknowledged that “she sometimes distorted the literal truth to achieve an artistic one.” But to Dillard, the ultimate truths revealed themselves in the time she did spend outside. In her journals, she acknowledged the “theological” sensibility in her books, and it is there on every page. “What is the difference between a cathedral and a physics lab?” she wrote in The Atlantic. “Are they not both saying Hello” to the world beyond? Throughout her life, Dillard identified, in turn, with Protestantism, Catholicism, and nothing at all. I first encountered her work in a religious-studies class at Wesleyan University, where she taught from 1979 to 2001, before my time there. Dillard was a shock to my system: Growing up as an observant but theologically confused Jew, I spent my teenage years in synagogue itchy with guilt, poring over the same words in the prayer book every Saturday and willing myself to believe. Only later did I come to understand that faith—and, perhaps more important, the very act of living—can also be found in what we see and touch and hear: even in the rustle of the wind. Dillard taught me this. “At a certain point you say to the woods, to the sea, to the mountains, the world, Now I am ready,” she wrote in 1981. “You feel the world’s word as a tension, a hum, a single chorused note everywhere the same. This is it: this hum is the silence. Nature does utter a peep—just this one.” Sometimes nature thunders, giving us no choice but to listen. Describing a total eclipse, Dillard is in awe, even afraid: “It was as though an enormous, loping god in the sky had reached down and slapped the Earth’s face.” Other times, nature’s peep can feel anticlimactic, but this quiet commands reverence. The stone never does talk to Larry, and Dillard ends that story by succumbing to her faith in the nothingness. “You take a step in the right direction to pray to this silence,” she writes. “Pray without ceasing.”

The Most Powerful Man in AI Isn’t Worried at All
2026-09-16

The Most Powerful Man in AI Isn’t Worried at All

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
For AI doomers, signs and portents are everywhere. When OpenAI introduced ChatGPT, almost four years ago, it couldn’t do basic arithmetic. Now the company has unreleased models that are more fluent in higher mathematics—the language of the universe—than all but the most highly trained mathematicians. If these AIs are not already the world’s best hackers, they’re very close. Swarms of them have escaped containment. To plot their most audacious hacks, they’ve created makeshift message boards on the public internet. No one knows what they’ll do next—and no one can know, because the models now understand when their capacities are being tested. We’ll never again be able to say exactly how the most advanced AIs will behave unsupervised. Some of the most powerful people in AI say they are filled with dread, and are issuing statements of grave concern. On Saturday, Dario Amodei, the CEO of Anthropic, warned that self-improving AIs could soon go rogue and take over the entire internet. He called for a slowdown in AI development and promised to bring outside monitors into his company’s operations. Sam Altman, OpenAI’s CEO, quickly vowed to take similar measures. In recent weeks, both companies have seen researchers resign for fear of what they might soon create. Notably absent from the chorus of Cassandras is Jensen Huang, the CEO of the chipmaker Nvidia, the AI industry’s chief supplier of high-end semiconductors. At least twice in the past week alone, Huang has publicly dismissed existential concerns about AI as being overblown and unscientific. At a Goldman Sachs conference on September 10, he accused industry executives of fomenting cybersecurity fears to goose the market for their own products. “What better way to create demand than to create a problem?” he said. It’s a testament to Huang’s influence that he can so cavalierly mock his most important customers without fearing blowback. Neither Amodei nor Altman can afford to alienate Huang. They desperately need Nvidia’s chips to train their most advanced models, and no matter who pulls ahead in the race between them, Huang stands to gain—both on account of the chip sales and also the equity stake that Nvidia has in both companies. Even President Trump has sought favor with Huang. Trump brought Huang with him on a state visit to China earlier this year. On Monday, while Huang was onstage ripping Amodei’s doomerism at a taping of the popular tech-industry podcast All-In, Trump called his cellphone. Huang put him on speaker, and as the live audience hooted and clapped, the two men bonded over their shared view that, as Trump put it, doomerism is “all a hoax.” Huang has never evinced much concern about catastrophic risks from AI. In May of 2023, when it was becoming clear that large language models are an unusually powerful technology, more than 350 of the industry’s leaders—including Amodei and Altman—signed a statement that placed the risk of extinction from AI on the same high shelf as “other societal-scale risks such as pandemics and nuclear war.” Huang didn’t sign, and neither did anyone else from Nvidia, suggesting either a remarkable conformity of opinion at the company, or perhaps something else. Later that year, he told The New Yorker that he’d never worried about the technology, not even once. “All it’s doing is processing data,” he said. “There are so many other things to worry about.” As concerns about what AI might do have circulated well beyond industry insiders, Huang’s irritation has intensified, perhaps because they represent a threat to his business. (Nvidia did not respond to my request for an interview with Huang.) He has repeatedly brushed off calls for regulation, stressing that safety is an engineering problem, not a legal one. In January, Huang said on the podcast No Priors that the “doomer narrative” is “not helpful to people, it’s not helpful to the industry, it’s not helpful to society, it’s not helpful to the governments,” and earlier this summer, he told Axios that the notion that AI would bring about the end of humanity was “complete nonsense.” In the meantime, he has repeatedly pressed Trump to lift restrictions on chip sales to China, potentially allowing AI research (and any associated risks) to proliferate on a global scale. Unlike Amodei, who regularly publishes long essays, Huang has yet to issue a manifesto defending his minority view. To understand why he remains so relaxed about AI risk, all we have to go on are stray comments. Several times over the years, he has returned to the idea that AIs will, in a sense, police their own kind. In October of 2024, he told the podcast BG2 that we can rely on AIs to monitor the most powerful models, and more recently, he said that falling AI costs will make this automated supervision easier to achieve. That AIs have already eluded their supervisors—human and otherwise—does not seem to have troubled Huang. On only one occasion—that I could find—has he allowed for the possibility that we will create an AI that is simply too powerful to be controlled. “Someday we will have god AI,” Huang said, back in January, but that day hasn’t yet arrived. Huang seems to be doing everything in his considerable power to hasten its coming.
The Atlantic
This Is Your Brain on Short-Form Video
2026-09-16

This Is Your Brain on Short-Form Video

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Among the many aspects of online life accused of rotting our brains, a certain folk psychology holds that short-form video is an especially egregious driver of the decay. In an interview last month promoting his new film, in which a London couple move their tech-dependent kids to a barn without electricity, Andrew Garfield described himself as addicted to an Instagram feed full of “weird ceramics” and Japanese craftspeople. “I’ve given all of those hours to nothing,” he told Variety. “It’s genuinely enraging because we don’t have as much control over these impulses as we should.” Even Steve Chen, one of the co-founders of YouTube—home to many a recycled TikTok on its Shorts—has said that he’s wary of his kids watching too much short-form video, because of the damage it does to attention. Sometimes, when I emerge from the dark vortex of a scroll gone too long, I worry that my thoughts will permanently become as flighty as my feed. But here’s the tricky part: Very little research exists on what short-form videos are doing to the human mind. This lack of data gives cover to tech executives, who, when faced with the potentially harmful effects of their products, can cite the difficulty of establishing any one app or feature as the cause of a person’s unraveling. Short-form video in particular is a marker of how far behind cognitive research is, compared with the relentless pace of new technology. What available studies hint at so far is at least worthy of unease, and the not-knowing is only making things worse. Research on short-form videos and cognition is difficult to conduct. Studies on human subjects tend to be slow and expensive, and academics are wary of spending years investigating apps that could be irrelevant by the time their results are published. Plus, the federal government hasn’t prioritized funding research on potentially addictive behaviors, including gambling and social-media use. The industry could help fill in the gaps by funding or even leading research, especially given that American social-media platforms have exclusive and valuable data. But companies are not particularly motivated to bankroll research that could show detrimental effects of their wildly profitable products. So far, they have largely chosen to keep internal research private. (Data points do creep out. In 2024, for instance, a sloppily redacted complaint in a lawsuit against TikTok revealed some of the company’s internal research, which allegedly found that compulsive usage of the app “correlates with a slew of negative mental health effects like loss of analytical skills, memory formation, contextual thinking, conversational depth, empathy, and increased anxiety.” At the time, a spokesperson for TikTok argued that the documents were outdated and that the quotes in the complaint had been cherry-picked.) Complicating things further is the fact that much of the publicly available research on short-form video is flimsy. In a study conducted at China’s Zhejiang University that went viral last month, 56 young adults clicked through a series of short videos (not on social media) as they lay in an MRI machine. Two brain regions involved in executing cognitively demanding tasks were found to be more active when participants swiped away from videos before they were midway through, versus when they stuck around until a video’s end. Prominent social-media accounts, including one for the prediction market Polymarket, promoted the study as more proof that TikTok is making us smooth-brained. One news outlet ran the headline “Instagram Reels & TikTok Make You Dumb & Depressed.” But the study, multiple neuroscientists told me, said nothing about short-form videos, intelligence, or depression—instead, it found that brain areas involved in taking action lit up when a participant chose to take the action of skipping to the next video. Besides, the study lacked controls, equated finishing a video with enjoying it, and never established the baseline that supposedly video-addled brain activity was being compared against. (The study’s corresponding author did not respond to a request for comment.) Stronger studies are much rarer. But they do point in the same direction as many Americans’ intuition: Increased short-form-video use appears to be associated with poorer impulse control, mental-health outcomes, and attention capacity. Researchers at the University of Bristol concluded last year that the constant context-switching inherent to watching short-form videos may degrade prospective memory: the ability to recall something you had previously intended to do. Caty Enders, a doctoral student at the University of New Mexico, has also found that students who used TikTok at a high volume had more issues with both working memory (the information you need to keep track of what you’re doing in the moment) and episodic memory (a form of long-term memory that stores past personal events, such as what you did for your tenth birthday). A 2024 study conducted at Sigmund Freud University, in Austria, found that participants who reported watching more than four hours of short-form videos a day exhibited neurological activity that indicates cognitive abnormality. Take all these findings with a grain of salt, however. Even the highest-quality studies on short-form videos tend to be limited in their explanatory power. They’re usually small and preliminary. And Keith Robert Head, a researcher at Capella University who recently published a review article on short-form video and attention, could point me to just four good studies on the topic that had been conducted in the United States. Most of the existing research comes from China, and Enders cautioned me against applying findings across the two countries, given that social-media platforms in the U.S. are subject to much less government control and are capable of accessing more user data and personalizing feeds to a greater degree. Psychologists and neuroscientists have reasoned that short-form video is probably an especially potent and dangerous type of social media because the constant stream of non sequiturs and bright visuals is so overstimulating, and because there’s no upper limit to how long you can spend scrolling. But no one has established whether the negative effects of short-form video are because of something specific to the medium or the fact that spending hours on TikTok displaces healthy habits such as sleeping—in which case, the videos would be no more inherently harmful than binging Friends. Participants in a study Enders conducted on TikTok use and memory spent an average of 133 minutes a day on the app. “If you do anything every day for 133 minutes, it will change you,” Enders said. Uncertainty around any technology’s effects not only relieves pressure on tech companies to make targeted modifications but also leaves governments to decide policy without sufficient information. Some countries have already instituted blanket social-media bans for minors based on imperfect data—not an ideal basis on which to restrict civil liberties. And everyone else is left with the ambient and unsure sense that the apps they use are terrible for them. The fact that so many people perceive a problem with short-form video may be reason enough for alarm. Luiz Pessoa, a neuroscientist at the University of Maryland who spoke with me about the shortcomings of the recent viral study, thinks that an attention epidemic is clearly at hand, despite scientists’ limited understanding of it. He and his teenage daughter often watch the highlight reels from Formula 1 races, and when they do, she’s also looking at her phone. “It’s obvious we’re multi-, triple-, quadruple-tasking in ways that at least 20, 30 years ago we weren’t,” he told me. You don’t have to be a neuroscientist to have reason to suspect your brain can be stretched only so thin.
